Since $\rho(g)v_i\in\rho(g)W_i$ and $f$ has mass at most one, Cauchy–Schwarz yields $$|\langle w,\widehat f(\rho)v_i\rangle|
 \le\|v_i\|\left(\sum_gf(g)\|\Pi_{\rho(g)W_i}w\|^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le\sqrt{BC_u}D^{-1/2+(u+2)/(2b)}\|w\|\|v_i\|.$$ For $v=\sum_i v_i$, orthogonality gives $\sum_i\|v_i\|\le\sqrt b\|v\|$. Summing proves the claim. In particular no dimension of a multiplicity space has been discarded. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-l-eight-convolutions"></a>
<div id="l:eight-convolutions" class="corollary">

**Corollary 9** (Four convolutions by the orbit argument). *<span id="l:orbit-completion" label="l:orbit-completion"></span>Let $n\to\infty$ through multiples of eight. Suppose probabilities $\mu_n$ on $S_n$ have sign mean $o(1)$ and $$\mathbb E_{|M|=n/8}\|\mu_{n,H_M}-U_{G/H_M}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1).$$ Then $\|\mu_n^{*4}-U_G\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\to0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-eight-convolutions"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-eight-convolutions</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#l:partition-selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:partition-selection">3</a> selects an eight-block partition with summed marginal error $o(1)$. Lemma <a href="#l:trim" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:trim">4</a> supplies a common subprobability $f_n\le\mu_n$ of mass $1-o(1)$ and coset cap one. Proposition <a href="#l:orbit-span" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:orbit-span">8</a> at $u=1$ gives $\|\widehat f_n(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\sqrt{8C_1}D^{-5/16}$. The nonsign Plancherel contribution after four factors is at most $(8C_1)^4 Z_{1/2}(n)$, because $2-8(5/16)=-1/2$. Sign and missing mass tend to zero by Proposition <a href="#l:amplification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:amplification">6</a>. This completes a route from averaged observations to the full law. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Central projections retain the whole Fourier matrix

The orbit argument controls one vector at a time. To estimate the Hilbert–Schmidt norm without paying an extra ambient dimension, we instead convolve with a central idempotent of the subgroup. Its cosetwise squared norm controls all Fourier matrix entries at once.

For an irreducible $H$-type $\tau$ of degree $a$, define the mass function $$e_{H,\tau}(h)=\frac{a}{|H|}\overline{\chi_\tau(h)}\quad(h\in H),\qquad e_{H,\tau}=0\text{ off }H.$$ Character orthogonality shows that $\widehat e_{H,\tau}(\rho)$ is the orthogonal projection $P_{\rho,H,\tau}$ onto the full $\tau$-isotypic subspace in $\rho|_H$. Thus every copy is included.

<a id="stmt-l-central-isotypic"></a>
<div id="l:central-isotypic" class="lemma">

<a id="l:central-global"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Cosetwise isotypic estimate). *Let $H\le G$ and let $f\ge0$ have mass $z\le1$ and satisfy $f(gH)\le B/[G:H]$. For an irreducible $H$-type $\tau$ of degree $a$, $$\label{l:central-global}
 \sum_{\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)}D_\rho\|\widehat f(\rho)P_{\rho,H,\tau}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le Bza^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-central-isotypic"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-central-isotypic</summary>

*Proof.* Convolution with $e=e_{H,\tau}$ acts separately in each left coset $Q=xH$. Since $\sum_{h\in H}|e(h)|^2=a^2/|H|$, the triangle inequality for a positive weighted sum of translates gives $$\sum_{h\in H}|(f*e)(xh)|^2\le f(Q)^2\frac{a^2}{|H|}.$$ One may equivalently normalize the weights by $f(Q)$ and use Jensen; the zero-mass coset contributes zero. Also $\sum_Q f(Q)^2\le Bz/[G:H]$. Plancherel on $G$ proves <a href="#l:central-global" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:central-global">[l:central-global]</a>.

There is a second exact expression for the same calculation. Let $f_Q(h)=f(xh)$ and $\widehat f_Q(\tau)=\sum_hf_Q(h)\tau(h)$. Subgroup Plancherel yields $$\sum_\rho D_\rho\|\widehat f(\rho)P_{\rho,H,\tau}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =[G:H]\sum_Q a\|\widehat f_Q(\tau)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.$$ Unitarity gives $\|\widehat f_Q(\tau)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le\sqrt a\,f(Q)$ and recovers the same bound. This identity makes the normalization of the Hilbert–Schmidt norm explicit.

There is also a positive-projection proof of the same bound. Right convolution by $e$ is an orthogonal projection, since $e*e=e$ and $e^*(h)=\overline{e(h^{-1})}=e(h)$. With the counting inner product, <a id="l:isotypic-positive-projection"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 |G|\|f*e\|_2^2
 &=|G|\langle f,f*e\rangle\notag\\
 &\le a^2|G|\langle f,f*U_H\rangle
 \le Bza^2.\label{l:isotypic-positive-projection}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed $|e(h)|\le a^2U_H(h)$, so positivity of $f$ bounds the absolute convolution pointwise; and $(f*U_H)(g)=f(gH)/|H|\le B/|G|$. This proves the estimate from idempotence and positivity, independently of the cosetwise norm calculation. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-l-isotypic"></a>
<div id="l:isotypic" class="proposition">

**Proposition 11** (Isotypic transfer). *Under the assumptions of Theorem <a href="#l:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:main">1</a>, $$\|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le bBC_uD^{-1+(u+2)/b}.$$ In fact the right side can be multiplied by $f(G)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-isotypic"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-isotypic</summary>

<a id="l:projection-cover"></a>
*Proof.* For each $i$, take all $H_i$-isotypic projections with degree at most $D^{1/b}$. On every product type in the restriction to $\prod_iH_i$, at least one such projection is identity. The projections commute and include all multiplicities, so $$\label{l:projection-cover}
 I\preceq\sum_{i=1}^b\sum_{\tau:\dim\tau\le D^{1/b}}P_{\rho,H_i,\tau}.$$ Trace this inequality against the positive operator $\widehat f(\rho)^*\widehat f(\rho)$, apply Lemma <a href="#l:central-isotypic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:central-isotypic">10</a> after discarding other ambient irreducibles, and use <a href="#l:orbit-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:orbit-dimension">[l:orbit-dimension]</a>. The resulting inequality is $D\|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le Bf(G)bC_uD^{(u+2)/b}$. ◻

</details>

This proves Theorem <a href="#l:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:main">1</a>. The improvement from operator to Hilbert–Schmidt norm changes the convolution count because the regular-representation weight is then $D$, rather than the $D^2$ obtained by bounding $\|A\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2$ by $D\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2$.

<a id="stmt-l-four-convolutions"></a>
<div id="l:four-convolutions" class="corollary">

**Corollary 12** (Two convolutions from eight complements). *<span id="l:isotypic-completion" label="l:isotypic-completion"></span>Under the hypotheses of Corollary <a href="#l:orbit-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:orbit-completion">[l:orbit-completion]</a>, $\|\mu_n^{*2}-U_G\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\to0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-four-convolutions"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-four-convolutions</summary>

*Proof.* Use the same partition and subprobability as in that corollary. Proposition <a href="#l:isotypic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:isotypic">11</a> with $u=1$ gives HS squared at most $8C_1D^{-5/8}$. Two powers have nonsign contribution at most $(8C_1)^2 Z_{1/4}(n)$, since $1-2(5/8)=-1/4$. Proposition <a href="#l:amplification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:amplification">6</a> handles sign and mass. ◻

</details>

The last corollary is an abstract consequence of the stated marginal hypotheses. A shuffle application must supply those hypotheses at its own time and preserve its own time convention; a numerical mixing statement is not part of this abstract deduction.

<a id="l:comparisons-section"></a>
### Coefficient spaces and finer fixed-partition estimates

The central-isotypic estimate already controls the whole Fourier matrix. This section compares three features of alternative fixed-coset arguments: evaluation in a coefficient space, pointwise character control, and the number of restriction types allowed by the ambient diagram. The first two give weaker bounds but require different intermediate estimates; the branching refinement retains information that the uniform constant $C_u$ suppresses. Throughout this section $X=M_1\sqcup\cdots\sqcup M_b$, $H_i=\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(M_i)$ fixes the complement, and $f\ge0$ has mass at most one and satisfies $f(gH_i)\le B/[G:H_i]$. The constants $C_u$ have the meaning fixed in the introduction.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Comparison of the information and the conclusions

The first three rows of Table <a href="#l:comparison-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:comparison-table">1</a> compare methods under the same fixed-coset hypothesis; the remaining rows locate transfers with different orientations, quantifiers, or conditional structure. A fixed family of coset bounds controls one common measure. An estimate averaged over omitted sets first needs a choice of partition or a permutation-dependent modification. An entropy inequality valid for every change of law can be applied to a rare coefficient event; an entropy estimate for the reference law alone cannot justify that step. For kernels, small quotient error must be supplemented by control of the full sign multiplicity operators.

<div id="l:comparison-table">

| Input or method              | Orientation and quantifier                           | Mass treatment                | Fourier conclusion                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
|:-----------------------------|:-----------------------------------------------------|:------------------------------|:-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Central isotypic projection  | $gH_i$, every coset of one law                       | Subprobability allowed        | HS squared $bBC_uD^{-1+(u+2)/b}$                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Coefficient-space projection | Same fixed cosets                                    | Same retained law             | HS squared $bB^2C_uD^{-1+(u+2)/b}$                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Pointwise character bound    | Same fixed cosets                                    | Same retained law             | HS squared $bB^2C_uD^{-1+(u+4)/b}$                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Two-sided observations       | Image and inverse-image cosets                       | Two retained factors          | Joint-type bound, Section <a href="#l:two-sided-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:two-sided-section">8</a>                                                                                                                              |
| Uniform random omitted set   | Average marginal TV error                            | Nearby conditional law        | Alternative retained laws; Sections <a href="#l:random-partition-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:random-partition-section">5</a> and <a href="#l:domains-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:domains-section">10</a> |
| Entropy on a common event    | Every law supported on that event                    | Condition on the event        | Tail $CD^{-1/256}$; Theorem <a href="#l:tilted-entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:tilted-entropy">40</a>                                                                                                                                 |
| Equivariant quotient kernels | Mean rows, or both rows and columns                  | Subkernels with explicit loss | Product-type HS bounds; Theorems <a href="#l:equivariant-sixteen" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:equivariant-sixteen">34</a> and <a href="#l:three-group" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:three-group">35</a>                     |
| Conditional half-products    | Inverse-image cap followed by conditional image caps | Product subprobabilities      | Operator $\sqrt{22}D^{-1/40}$; Theorem <a href="#l:tabloid-product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:tabloid-product">52</a>                                                                                                                    |

Inputs that lead to different Fourier transfers. The dimension is that of the ambient irreducible except in the kernel product-type estimates. All Hilbert–Schmidt norms are unnormalized.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Evaluation of coefficient functions

<a id="stmt-l-coefficient-evaluation"></a>
<div id="l:coefficient-evaluation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Evaluation in a coefficient space). *Let $H$ be a finite group and let $\Lambda\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{Irr}}(H)$. If $F$ is a linear combination of matrix coefficients of the types in $\Lambda$, then $$\max_{h\in H}|F(h)|^2\le\left(\sum_{\tau\in\Lambda}(\dim\tau)^2\right)\mathbb E_{h\sim U_H}|F(h)|^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-coefficient-evaluation"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-coefficient-evaluation</summary>

*Proof.* Schur orthogonality makes the functions $\sqrt{\dim\tau}\,\tau(h)_{jk}$ an orthonormal basis of this coefficient space. At every $h$, the sum of their squared absolute values is $\sum_{\tau\in\Lambda}(\dim\tau)^2$, because each $\tau(h)$ is unitary. Cauchy–Schwarz for expansion in this basis proves the assertion. Multiple copies of a representation give the same coefficient functions and do not enlarge the space. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-l-coefficient-operator"></a>
<div id="l:coefficient-operator" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14** (Coefficient-evaluation transfer). *For every $u>0$ and irreducible $\rho$ of degree $D$, $$\|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\sqrt{bBC_u}\,D^{-1/2+(u+2)/(2b)}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-coefficient-operator"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-coefficient-operator</summary>

*Proof.* Let $P_i$ project onto all $H_i$-types of degree at most $D^{1/b}$. The $P_i$ commute, and $\prod_i(I-P_i)=0$ by the product-degree bound. Thus $Q_i=P_i\prod_{j<i}(I-P_j)$ are pairwise orthogonal projections summing to identity. Write $v_i=Q_iv$.

For fixed $g,w$, the function $h\mapsto\langle w,\rho(gh)v_i\rangle$ on $H_i$ uses only the selected types. Lemma <a href="#l:coefficient-evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:coefficient-evaluation">13</a> gives $$|\langle w,\rho(g)v_i\rangle|^2
 \le C_uD^{(u+2)/b}\mathbb E_{h\sim U_{H_i}}|\langle w,\rho(gh)v_i\rangle|^2.$$ Average over $g$ with mass $f(g)$. Right averaging replaces $f$ by $f*U_{H_i}$, whose density relative to $U_G$ is at most $B$. Schur orthogonality on $G$ gives $$\sum_gf(g)|\langle w,\rho(g)v_i\rangle|^2
 \le BC_uD^{-1+(u+2)/b}\|w\|^2\|v_i\|^2.$$ Jensen for a measure of mass at most one and $\sum_i\|v_i\|\le\sqrt b\|v\|$ finish the proof. This argument proves the same numerical inequality as Proposition <a href="#l:orbit-span" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:orbit-span">8</a>, using evaluation of functions instead of dimensions of orbit subspaces. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Two more isotypic estimates

<a id="stmt-l-isotypic-alternatives"></a>
<div id="l:isotypic-alternatives" class="lemma">

<a id="l:coefficient-isotypic"></a>
<a id="l:pointwise-isotypic"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Coefficient and pointwise character estimates). *Let $H\le G$, let $f\ge0$ have mass at most one and coset cap $B/[G:H]$, and let $\tau\in\mathop{\mathrm{Irr}}(H)$ have degree $a$. For every $\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)$, $$\begin{aligned}
 D_\rho\|\widehat f(\rho)P_{\rho,H,\tau}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2&\le B^2a^2,\label{l:coefficient-isotypic}\\
 D_\rho\|\widehat f(\rho)P_{\rho,H,\tau}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2&\le B^2a^4.\label{l:pointwise-isotypic}
\end{aligned}$$ The first bound comes from coefficient projection on each coset; the second comes from a pointwise bound on the character idempotent.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-isotypic-alternatives"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-isotypic-alternatives</summary>

*Proof.* Put $F=|G|f$. On $xH$, project $h\mapsto F(xh)$ onto the conjugates of the matrix coefficients of $\tau$. Its uniform squared norm is $$a\left\|\mathbb E_{h\in H}F(xh)\tau(h)\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le B^2a^2,$$ because the matrix in the norm has operator norm at most the nonnegative function’s mean, which is at most $B$. The entries of $\rho(xh)P_{\rho,H,\tau}$ belong to the $\tau$ coefficient space. Replacing $F$ by this projection therefore preserves the tested Fourier matrix. Averaging the bound over cosets and applying full-group Plancherel proves <a href="#l:coefficient-isotypic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:coefficient-isotypic">[l:coefficient-isotypic]</a>.

For the second proof, the character bound $|\chi_\tau(h)|\le a$ gives $$|(f*e_{H,\tau})(g)|\le\frac{a^2}{|H|}f(gH)\le\frac{Ba^2}{|G|}.$$ The density of this convolution has squared $L^2(U_G)$ norm at most $B^2a^4$. Plancherel gives <a href="#l:pointwise-isotypic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:pointwise-isotypic">[l:pointwise-isotypic]</a>. No bound on the conditional law inside a coset was required in either proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-l-coarse-character-lift"></a>
<div id="l:coarse-character-lift" class="proposition">

<a id="l:coarse-hs"></a>
**Proposition 16** (Coefficient and character Hilbert–Schmidt transfers). *<span id="l:coefficient-lift" label="l:coefficient-lift"></span>For the disjoint-block data of this section and every $u>0$, <a id="l:coefficient-hs"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2&\le B^2bC_uD^{-1+(u+2)/b},\label{l:coefficient-hs}\\
 \|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2&\le B^2bC_uD^{-1+(u+4)/b}.\label{l:coarse-hs}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-coarse-character-lift"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-coarse-character-lift</summary>

*Proof.* Trace the covering inequality <a href="#l:projection-cover" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:projection-cover">[l:projection-cover]</a> against $\widehat f^*\widehat f$. Sum the corresponding estimate of Lemma <a href="#l:isotypic-alternatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:isotypic-alternatives">15</a> over all selected subgroup types. For $j=2,4$, $$\sum_{\dim\tau\le D^{1/b}}(\dim\tau)^j
 \le C_uD^{(u+j)/b}.$$ This proves both bounds, including every multiplicity. ◻

</details>

Although <a href="#l:central-global" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:central-global">[l:central-global]</a> gives a stronger bound, <a href="#l:coarse-hs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:coarse-hs">[l:coarse-hs]</a> records the method that uses only pointwise character control. For $u=2$, large block size makes the complete subgroup reciprocal sum at most three, so cap $B=4$ gives the concrete bound $48bD^{-1+6/b}$.

<a id="stmt-l-central-projector-operator"></a>
<div id="l:central-projector-operator" class="proposition">

**Proposition 17** (Low-type central-projector estimates). *Let $\eta$ be a probability satisfying the coset caps of this section. Let $P_i$ select its $H_i$-types of degree at most $D^{1/b}$ and put $R_i=\sum_{\dim\tau\le D^{1/b}}(\dim\tau)^2$. Then $$\|\widehat\eta(\rho)P_i\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le BD^{-1/2}R_i,
 \qquad D\|\widehat\eta(\rho)P_i\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le B^2R_i.$$ Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
 \|\widehat\eta(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}&\le BC_u\sqrt b\,D^{-1/2+(u+2)/b},\\
 \|\widehat\eta(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}&\le B\sqrt{bC_u}\,D^{-1/2+(u+2)/(2b)}.
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-central-projector-operator"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-central-projector-operator</summary>

*Proof.* On $H_i$ take the central projector density $k_i(h)=\sum_{\dim\tau\le D^{1/b}}(\dim\tau)\overline{\chi_\tau(h)}$, relative to uniform subgroup measure. Its transform is $P_i$. Pointwise $|k_i|\le R_i$, so convolution of the density $|G|\eta$ with $k_i$ is bounded in absolute value by $BR_i$. Plancherel yields the first displayed bound. Alternatively project onto all selected coefficient spaces on each coset. They are orthogonal; their squared mean sums to at most $B^2R_i$ by the first proof of Lemma <a href="#l:isotypic-alternatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:isotypic-alternatives">15</a>. This gives the second bound.

Use the orthogonal $Q_i=P_i\prod_{j<i}(I-P_j)$, apply either bound to $Q_iv$, and sum their norms. The inequalities $\sum_i\|Q_iv\|\le\sqrt b\|v\|$ and $R_i\le C_uD^{(u+2)/b}$ give the conclusions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Counting only types allowed by branching

Let $p(j)$ again denote the partition number, and define $M(k)=\sum_{j=0}^kp(j)$. For $\lambda\vdash n$ put $k_\lambda=n-\max(\lambda_1,\lambda'_1)$.

<a id="stmt-l-branching-hs"></a>
<div id="l:branching-hs" class="proposition">

**Proposition 18** (Branching refinement). *For an irreducible $\rho_\lambda$ of degree $D$, the disjoint-block caps give $$\|\widehat f(\rho_\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le BbM(k_\lambda)D^{-1+2/b}.$$ The blocks may have unequal sizes.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-branching-hs"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-branching-hs</summary>

*Proof.* Young branching says that each diagram $\tau$ occurring under restriction to a block symmetric group is contained in $\lambda$; conjugating a block to the standard subgroup does not change the set of occurring types <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[James1978](#ref-James1978), Theorem 9.2]. Orient a longest row or column of $\lambda$ horizontally, transposing the restricted diagrams too if necessary. Each $\tau$ then has at most $k_\lambda$ boxes below its first row. Given those lower boxes and the block size, its first row is determined. Thus at most $M(k_\lambda)$ types occur, regardless of multiplicity. Apply <a href="#l:central-global" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:central-global">[l:central-global]</a> to each selected small type and bound its squared degree by $D^{2/b}$ in <a href="#l:projection-cover" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:projection-cover">[l:projection-cover]</a>. ◻

</details>

Appendix <a href="#l:transfer-supplement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:transfer-supplement">18</a> develops two averages of low-type projections, a four-factor coefficient decomposition, and explicit parameter substitutions. Those alternative estimates retain their proofs, while the tools above suffice for the transfers that follow.

<a id="l:random-partition-section"></a>
### Keeping the partition random

Selecting one partition and imposing common coset caps already handles the averaged marginal hypothesis. Here we construct a different retained law: each retained permutation is good for many partitions, without choosing one partition on which the law must satisfy every cap. The partition therefore varies inside the coefficient estimate. Its event of simultaneous goodness must be inserted before decomposing a test vector.

<a id="stmt-l-random-partition"></a>
<div id="l:random-partition" class="proposition">

<a id="l:random-op"></a>
**Proposition 19** (Random-partition lift). *Let $n$ tend to infinity through multiples of a fixed integer $q>3$, and let $\mu$ be probability laws on $S_n$. For a uniform $n/q$-subset $C$, put $H_C=\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(C)$, fixing its complement pointwise. Suppose $$\mathbb E_C\|\mu_{H_C}-(U_G)_{H_C}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1),
 \qquad \widehat\mu(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})=0.$$ There is a conditional probability law $\nu$ with $\|\nu-\mu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1)$ such that, for every irreducible $\rho$ of degree $D$, $$\label{l:random-op}
 \|\widehat\nu(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 \le \frac2M\sqrt{2qC_1}\,D^{-(1-3/q)/2},$$ where $M=1-o(1)$ is the retained mass and $C_1$ is from Lemma <a href="#l:degree-basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:degree-basic">2</a>. In particular for $q=8$, $\|\mu^{*8}-U_G\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=o(1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-random-partition"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-random-partition</summary>

*Proof.* Put $h_0=|H_C|/|G|$. Say $g$ is good for $C$ if $\mu(gH_C)\le2h_0$. The total $\mu$-mass of bad cosets is at most twice their marginal total-variation distance. Thus the set $$R=\{g:\mathbb P_C(g\text{ is bad for }C)\le1/(2q)\}$$ has mass $M=1-o(1)$ by Markov’s inequality. For every $g\in R$ a uniform ordered equal partition $(C_1',\ldots,C_q')$ has probability at least $1/2$ that $g$ is good for all blocks. Define $\nu=\mu(\cdot\mid R)$.

<a id="l:random-evaluation"></a>
We use Lemma <a href="#l:coefficient-evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:coefficient-evaluation">13</a>: if a function $F$ on a finite group is in the span of matrix coefficients of a collection $\Lambda$ of irreducibles, then $$\label{l:random-evaluation}
 \max|F|^2\le
 \left(\sum_{\tau\in\Lambda}(\dim\tau)^2\right)
                     \mathbb E_U|F|^2.$$ The sum counts distinct types, since equivalent copies have the same space of coefficient functions.

<a id="l:good-coset-square"></a>
Fix a partition and restrict $\rho$ to its direct product of block subgroups. Decompose orthogonally into tensor-product constituents, including all multiplicity spaces. Every constituent has product of factor degrees at most $D$. Assign it to one factor with degree at most $D^{1/q}$. This splits a vector $v=\sum_i v_i$ orthogonally, with $v_i$ using only $H_{C_i'}$-types of degrees at most $D^{1/q}$. Fix a test vector $w$ and set $F_i(g)=\langle w,\rho(g)v_i\rangle$. On a coset $gH_{C_i'}$ it belongs to the coefficient space in <a href="#l:random-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:random-evaluation">[l:random-evaluation]</a>, whose dimension is at most $$\sum_{b\le D^{1/q}}b^2\le C_1D^{3/q}.$$ The sum counts each irreducible type once; multiplicities do not increase the function space of matrix coefficients. Summing the coset maximum bound only over good cosets, and using Schur orthogonality on $G$, yields $$\label{l:good-coset-square}
 \sum_{g\text{ good for }C_i'}\mu(g)|F_i(g)|^2
 \le2C_1D^{3/q-1}\|w\|^2\|v_i\|^2.$$ Only a bound on total coset mass was used here.

For $g\in R$ insert the all-good partition event, whose probability is at least $1/2$, before splitting the coefficient. It follows that $$|\langle w,\widehat\nu(\rho)v\rangle|
 \le\frac2M\mathbb E_{(C_i')}
      \sum_i\sum_{g\text{ good for }C_i'}\mu(g)|F_i(g)|.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz and <a href="#l:good-coset-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:good-coset-square">[l:good-coset-square]</a>, followed by $\sum_i\|v_i\|\le\sqrt q\|v\|$, prove <a href="#l:random-op" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:random-op">[l:random-op]</a>. The dependence of $v_i$ on the partition is harmless because the estimate holds for each fixed partition.

The sign coefficient obeys $|\widehat\nu(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})|\le(1-M)/M=o(1)$, since the original sign coefficient is zero. The same argument works when that coefficient is $o(1)$. Fourier Plancherel in the normalization of Lemma <a href="#l:plancherel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:plancherel">5</a> gives $$4\|\nu^{*r}-U_G\|_{\mathrm{TV}}^2
 \le\sum_{\rho\ne\mathbf1}D_\rho
                    \|\widehat\nu(\rho)^r\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.$$ For $q=r=8$, the nonsign sum is at most an absolute constant times $$\sum_{\rho\ne\mathbf1,\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}}
           D_\rho^{2-r(1-3/q)}
 =\sum_{\rho\ne\mathbf1,\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}}D_\rho^{-3}=o(1)$$ by Lemma <a href="#l:degree-basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:degree-basic">2</a>. The sign term also vanishes, and $\|\mu^{*8}-\nu^{*8}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\le8(1-M)=o(1)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="l:degrees"></a>
### Symmetric-group degrees: the quantitative toolkit

<span id="l:degrees-section" label="l:degrees-section"></span>

The lifting estimates involve sums of negative powers of dimensions. The short proof in Lemma <a href="#l:degree-basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:degree-basic">2</a> already supplies every positive inverse power. Here we develop the quantitative estimates used by the later transfers: tableau and hook constructions, exponential growth in the deficit from a longest line, and sharper bounds near a row or column.

Theorem <a href="#l:degree-all-powers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:degree-all-powers">22</a> combines summability with the uniform deficit bound. Lemmas <a href="#l:degree-inverse-first" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:degree-inverse-first">23</a> and <a href="#l:degree-type-absorption" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:degree-type-absorption">24</a> then control near-row dimensions and the number of restriction types. Appendix <a href="#l:degree-supplement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:degree-supplement">16</a> retains the independent fixed-power proofs, including routes that use only coarse partition counts or avoid the hook formula.

For a partition $\lambda\vdash n$, write $D_\lambda=f^\lambda$ for the dimension of its complex irreducible representation and $\lambda'$ for its transpose. We use the classical standard-tableau dimension theorem and hook-length formula <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[sagan](#ref-sagan), [etingof](#ref-etingof)]: $$D_\lambda=\#\{\text{standard tableaux of shape }\lambda\}
 =\frac{n!}{\prod_{x\in\lambda}h(x)}.$$ We set $D_{\varnothing}=1$ and $\binom a{-1}=0$. A standard tableau increases along rows and columns; $h(x)$ is one plus the number of boxes strictly to the right of or below $x$. Transposition preserves dimension. Put $$L(\lambda)=\max(\lambda_1,\lambda'_1),\qquad
 k(\lambda)=n-L(\lambda),\qquad
 Z_u(n)=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n,\ \lambda\notin\{(n),(1^n)\}}
                     D_\lambda^{-u}.$$ The excluded diagrams are a set, so coincide when $n=1$. They are exactly the one-dimensional types: transpositions are conjugate and generate $S_n$, so a one-dimensional character takes the same value $1$ or $-1$ on all transpositions.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Counting constructions

<a id="l:partition-counts"></a>
Let $p(j)$ be the number of partitions of $j$, with $p(0)=1$. We will use each of the elementary bounds $$\label{l:partition-counts}
 p(j)\le2^{j-1}\le2^j,\qquad
 p(j)\le(j+1)^{2\lceil\sqrt j\rceil},\qquad
 p(j)\le e^{3\sqrt j}\quad(j\ge1).$$ The first follows by counting compositions. For the second, record the multiplicities of parts at most $\lceil\sqrt j\rceil$ and a zero-padded list of the at most $\sqrt j$ larger parts. Each entry has at most $j+1$ possibilities. For the third, evaluate the partition generating product at $x=e^{-1/\sqrt j}$. Its logarithm is $$\sum_{r\ge1}\frac1{r(e^{r/\sqrt j}-1)}
 \le\sqrt j\sum_{r\ge1}r^{-2}\le2\sqrt j,$$ and extracting the coefficient of $x^j$ costs $e^{\sqrt j}$. At a fixed positive value of $k(\lambda)$ there are at most $2p(k)$ shapes, since deleting a longest row or column leaves a partition of $k$. Also $L(\lambda)\ge\sqrt n$, because the diagram is contained in a square of side $L(\lambda)$.

<a id="stmt-l-tableau-constructions"></a>
<div id="l:tableau-constructions" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (Tableau constructions). *Suppose a diagram has first row of length $a$, lower diagram $\gamma$ of size $k$, and second row of length $c$ (put $c=0$ if $k=0$).*

1.  *Every tableau on a Young subdiagram extends to the whole diagram. For $0\le b\le\min(a,k)$, <a id="l:tableau-interleave"></a>$$\label{l:tableau-interleave}
     D_\lambda\ge\binom ab,
     \qquad D_\lambda\ge\binom{a+k-c}{k}.$$*

<a id="l:tableau-ballot"></a>
2.  *If $k\le a$, ballot interleavings give $$\label{l:tableau-ballot}
     D_\lambda\ge\binom{a+k}{k}-\binom{a+k}{k-1}
     =\binom{a+k}{k}\frac{a-k+1}{a+1}
     \ge\frac1{k+1}\binom{2k}{k}.$$ In particular a two-row rectangle of width $b$ has $\binom{2b}{b}/(b+1)$ tableaux.*

3.  *If the nonempty diagonals of constant row-plus-column index have sizes $t_1,\ldots,t_q$, then <a id="l:tableau-diagonal"></a>$$\label{l:tableau-diagonal}
     D_\lambda\ge\prod_{j=1}^q t_j!
     \ge2^{n-q},\qquad
     D_\lambda\ge\left(\frac n{qe}\right)^n.$$ Thus width and height at most $r$ give $q\le2r-1$ and $D_\lambda\ge2^{n-2r+1}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-tableau-constructions"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-tableau-constructions</summary>

*Proof.* A Young subdiagram is closed under moving up and left. Add its remaining boxes in row-major order with successive larger labels; every predecessor is already present. To prove the first bound, retain the whole first row and a Young subdiagram of $b$ lower boxes. Such a subdiagram is obtained by removing lower corners. Fill the first $b$ top boxes with $1,\ldots,b$; choose the $b$ lower labels from the remaining $a$ labels and fill them in the relative order of one fixed standard tableau. All lower boxes lie in the first $b$ columns, so filling the rest of the top row increasingly is valid. For the second bound, reserve only the first $c$ top labels and choose all $k$ lower labels from the remaining $a+k-c$ labels.

For the ballot bound, fix a standard order of the lower boxes and interleave their additions with those of the top row. Require each prefix to have at least as many top-row additions as lower additions. The $j$th lower box lies in a column at most $j$, so its top predecessor has been inserted. Reflection at the first violating prefix counts the valid words by the displayed binomial difference. A valid balanced word with $k$ additions of each kind, followed by $a-k$ top additions, gives the last bound. For an actual two-row rectangle the word condition is also necessary, proving the exact count.

Finally, put successive batches of labels on increasing diagonals, in arbitrary order within each diagonal. Every row or column predecessor lies on an earlier diagonal. This gives the product of factorials. The first estimate uses $t!\ge2^{t-1}$; the second uses $t!\ge(t/e)^t$ and convexity of $t\log t$ to obtain $\sum_jt_j\log t_j\ge n\log(n/q)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-l-degree-hook-ratio"></a>
<div id="l:degree-hook-ratio" class="lemma">

<a id="l:hook-rearrangement"></a>
<a id="l:hook-short-tail"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Separating the first-row hooks). *With the notation of Lemma <a href="#l:tableau-constructions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:tableau-constructions">20</a>, let $b_j$ be the number of lower boxes in column $j$. Then <a id="l:hook-exact"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 D_\lambda
 &=\binom{a+k}{k}D_\gamma
       \prod_{j=1}^a\left(1+\frac{b_j}{a-j+1}\right)^{-1}
       \label{l:hook-exact}\\
 &\ge\binom{a+k}{k}D_\gamma
       \exp\left(-\frac{k(1+\log a)}a\right).
       \label{l:hook-rearrangement}
\end{aligned}$$ If $k\le a$, a second lower bound is $$\label{l:hook-short-tail}
 D_\lambda\ge\binom{a+k}{k}D_\gamma
                \exp\left(-\frac{k}{a-k+1}\right).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-degree-hook-ratio"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-degree-hook-ratio</summary>

*Proof.* The lower hooks are unchanged, and the top hooks are $a-j+1+b_j$, which proves the identity. The sequence $b_j$ decreases while $(a-j+1)^{-1}$ increases. The opposite-order rearrangement inequality therefore gives $$\sum_{j=1}^a\frac{b_j}{a-j+1}
 \le\frac{k}{a}\sum_{j=1}^a\frac1j
 \le\frac{k(1+\log a)}a.$$ For completeness, the rearrangement inequality follows by expanding $\sum_{i,j}(b_i-b_j)(c_i-c_j)\le0$ for oppositely ordered sequences $b_i,c_i$. Apply $\log(1+x)\le x$ to the product. For the last estimate, $b_j=0$ for $j>k$, so every relevant denominator is at least $a-k+1$, and $\sum_jb_j=k$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### All positive powers and the first-row deficit

<a id="stmt-l-degree-all-powers"></a>
<div id="l:degree-all-powers" class="theorem">

<a id="l:degree-tail-exponential"></a>
**Theorem 22** (Every positive inverse power). *For every fixed real $u>0$, <a id="l:degree-all-powers-equation"></a>$$\label{l:degree-all-powers-equation}
 C_u:=\sup_{n\ge1}\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}D_\lambda^{-u}<\infty,
 \qquad Z_u(n)\longrightarrow0.$$ Moreover there are absolute constants $c_1,c_2>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n$ and every $\lambda\vdash n$, $$\label{l:degree-tail-exponential}
 D_\lambda\ge c_1e^{c_2k(\lambda)}.$$ For each fixed positive $k$, these dimensions tend uniformly to infinity among shapes with $k(\lambda)=k$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-degree-all-powers"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-degree-all-powers</summary>

*Proof.* Put $L=L(\lambda)$ and $k=n-L$. If $L\le n/16$, every hook is at most $2L$, so $D_\lambda\ge(n/(2eL))^n\ge2^n$. Otherwise orient the diagram with first row $L$, and for $k\ge1$ retain $b=\min(k,\lfloor L/3\rfloor)$ lower boxes. For sufficiently large $n$, $b\ge1$, and Lemma <a href="#l:tableau-constructions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:tableau-constructions">20</a> gives $$D_\lambda\ge\binom Lb\ge(L/b)^b\ge3^b,
 \qquad b\ge k/48-1.$$ Indeed $L>n/16$ implies $\lfloor L/3\rfloor>n/48-1\ge k/48-1$. Thus one may take $c_1=1/3$ and $c_2=(\log3)/48$ after enlarging the size threshold. For a fixed positive $k$, eventually $b=k$, and $D_\lambda\ge\binom{n-k}{k}\to\infty$ uniformly.

There are at most $2p(k)$ shapes at deficit $k$. By <a href="#l:partition-counts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:partition-counts">[l:partition-counts]</a> and <a href="#l:degree-tail-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:degree-tail-exponential">[l:degree-tail-exponential]</a>, their total inverse $u$th powers are bounded by a constant depending on $u$ times $e^{3\sqrt k-uc_2k}$. This is summable in $k$, and each fixed positive-$k$ contribution tends to zero. Dominated convergence proves $Z_u(n)\to0$. The full sum tends to two; its finitely many initial values are finite, including the value one at $n=1$. ◻

</details>

There are two other ways to obtain the full positive-power range. The next proof uses ballot words; the short proof from Lemma <a href="#l:degree-basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:degree-basic">2</a> uses a central-binomial subdiagram.

<details id="proof-stmt-l-degree-all-powers-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-degree-all-powers</summary>

*A ballot proof of the summability assertions.* For $k\ge1$ and $L=n-k\ge n/2$, <a href="#l:tableau-ballot" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:tableau-ballot">[l:tableau-ballot]</a> gives $$D_\lambda\ge\binom nk\frac{n-2k+1}{n-k+1}
       \ge\frac{2^k}{k+1}.$$ For the second inequality, the ballot fraction is at least $1/(k+1)$ and $\binom nk\ge(n/k)^k\ge2^k$. The majorant $2p(k)(k+1)^u2^{-uk}$ is summable, and the first binomial expression diverges for each fixed $k\ge1$. If $L<n/2$ but $L\ge n/10$, retain a row of length $L$ and $L$ lower boxes. The ballot count $\binom{2L}L/(L+1)$ is exponential in $n$. If $L<n/10$, the hook formula gives $(5/e)^n$. The subexponential bound for $p(n)$ handles both remaining ranges. Adding the one-dimensional terms proves the uniform bound. ◻

</details>

The third all-positive-power route is the one already proved in Lemma <a href="#l:degree-basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:degree-basic">2</a>. Its three ranges are $1\le k\le n/3$, $k>n/3$ with $L\ge n/10$, and $L<n/10$. The respective bounds are $\binom{n-k}k\ge2^k$, $2^L/(L+1)$, and $(5/e)^n$. Thus it uses the same initial-row construction with a central-binomial subdiagram, rather than the deficit-exponential or ballot estimates.

<a id="stmt-l-degree-inverse-first"></a>
<div id="l:degree-inverse-first" class="lemma">

<a id="l:degree-first-row-bound"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Near-row dimensions and inverse-first summability). *For $1\le k=k(\lambda)\le n/4$, with $\gamma$ the lower diagram after orienting a longest line as the first row, $$\label{l:degree-first-row-bound}
 D_\lambda\ge e^{-1/2}\binom nkD_\gamma
                  \ge\tfrac12\binom nk.$$ For $k>n/4$ and all sufficiently large $n$, $D_\lambda\ge e^{n/100}$. These estimates independently give $Z_1(n)\to0$ and $C_1<\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-degree-inverse-first"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-degree-inverse-first</summary>

*Proof.* Equation <a href="#l:hook-short-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:hook-short-tail">[l:hook-short-tail]</a> has exponent $k/(n-2k+1)\le1/2$, proving <a href="#l:degree-first-row-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:degree-first-row-bound">[l:degree-first-row-bound]</a>. If $k>n/4$ and $L\ge n/8$, keep the first row and $b=\lfloor n/32\rfloor$ lower boxes. For large $n$, $1\le b\le L/4$. The same hook bound gives dimension at least $\frac12\binom{L+b}b\ge4^b/2$. If $L<n/8$, every hook is shorter than $n/4$, giving $(4/e)^n$. Both exceed $e^{n/100}$ for sufficiently large $n$. The near-row inverse sum is bounded by $4\sum_{1\le k\le n/4}p(k)/\binom nk$. Each term vanishes at fixed $k$ and is bounded by $4p(k)4^{-k}$; the remaining contribution is at most $p(n)e^{-n/100}=o(1)$. This proves both assertions about inverse-first sums. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-l-degree-type-absorption"></a>
<div id="l:degree-type-absorption" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Absorbing the number of tail types). *Let $M(k)=\sum_{j=0}^kp(j)$. For every fixed $B\ge1$ and all sufficiently large $n$, uniformly over $\lambda\vdash n$ with $k(\lambda)\ge1$, <a id="l:degree-type-absorption-equation"></a>$$\label{l:degree-type-absorption-equation}
 4B M(k(\lambda))\le D_\lambda^{1/4}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-degree-type-absorption"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-degree-type-absorption</summary>

*Proof.* The partition bounds give $\log M(k)=O(\sqrt k\log(k+1))=o(k)$. For $k>n/4$, Lemma <a href="#l:degree-inverse-first" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:degree-inverse-first">23</a> gives $\log D_\lambda\ge n/100$, whereas $\log M(k)=o(n)$ uniformly for $k\le n$. For $k\le n/4$, use $D_\lambda\ge\frac12\binom nk\ge\frac12 4^k$ once $k$ exceeds a fixed sufficiently large cutoff. The finitely many positive $k$ below that cutoff are handled by the uniform divergence of $\binom nk$. One size threshold works for all shapes. ◻

</details>

<a id="l:characters-section"></a>
### One-dimensional characters in subgroups of controlled index

A coset cap also controls a Fourier projection obtained from a one-dimensional subgroup character. This gives a different route to the full isotypic estimates: first find one nonzero character space, then average it over conjugates. The construction here assigns boxes to rows or columns. Appendix <a href="#l:transfer-supplement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:transfer-supplement">18</a> gives an independent construction by successively removing longest lines, together with its reciprocal-degree proof and transfer.

<a id="stmt-l-small-index-character"></a>
<div id="l:small-index-character" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (A character from a row–column assignment). *For every irreducible complex representation of $S_n$, of degree $D$, there are a subgroup $J\le S_n$ and a character $\chi:J\to\{1,-1\}$ occurring in its restriction such that $$[S_n:J]\le D^{1024}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-small-index-character"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-small-index-character</summary>

*Proof.* We use the polytabloid realization of the Specht module of shape $\lambda$; see <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[sagan](#ref-sagan), [etingof](#ref-etingof)]. Fix a labeling of its boxes. A row tabloid remembers which labels lie in each row while forgetting their order within that row; its polytabloid is the signed sum of its translates under the column stabilizer. Assign every box either to its row or to its column. Let $R$ permute the assigned set in each row and let $C$ permute the assigned set in each column. These sets are disjoint, so $J=R\times C$. Give $R$ the trivial character and each factor of $C$ the sign character.

Let $e$ be the polytabloid of the labeled diagram. It transforms by sign under the full column stabilizer, hence under $C$. The average $v=|R|^{-1}\sum_{r\in R}re$ is fixed by $R$ and still transforms by sign under $C$. It is nonzero. Indeed the original row tabloid has coefficient one in $e$, since the full row and column stabilizers intersect trivially. Every element of $R$ fixes that tabloid, so its coefficient is also one in $v$. This proves character occurrence for every assignment.

<a id="l:assignment-rough-index"></a>
It remains to choose the assignment with controlled index. The arm and leg of a box count boxes strictly to its right and strictly below it. Assign a box to its row when its arm is at least its leg, and to its column otherwise. Its hook length is at most twice the length $l$ of the line receiving it. If that line receives $a$ boxes, then $a!\ge(a/e)^a$; we give a line with $a=0$ zero contribution below. Consequently $$\log\frac{\prod_{x\in\lambda}h(x)}
                  {\prod_{\text{assigned lines}}a!}
 \le n\log2+\sum_{\text{lines}}\{a+a\log(l/a)\}
 \le(\log2+1+2/e)n\le3n.$$ Here $a\log(l/a)\le l/e$ and the total length of all rows and columns is $2n$. The hook-length formula therefore gives an assignment with index $$\label{l:assignment-rough-index}
 I=[S_n:J]\le D e^{3n}.$$

We convert the exponential factor into a power of $D$. Put $L=\max(\lambda_1,\lambda'_1)$. Suppose first that $n\ge64$ and $L\le3n/4$. If $L\le n/8$, all hooks are at most $2L$, and $D\ge(n/(2eL))^n\ge(4/e)^n\ge e^{n/64}$. Otherwise transpose so that the first row has length $L$, and retain a Young order ideal of $s=\lfloor L/4\rfloor$ boxes below it. Such an ideal exists because $n-L\ge L/3$; moreover $s\ge n/64$. For the retained diagram, let $c_j$ count its lower boxes in column $j$. Dividing its hook formula by the lower diagram’s formula gives $$D\ge\binom{L+s}s
       \prod_{j=1}^L\left(1+\frac{c_j}{L-j+1}\right)^{-1}
 \ge\binom{L+s}s\exp\left(-\frac{s}{L-s+1}\right).$$ The first inequality also uses extension of tableaux from a subdiagram. For the second, $c_j=0$ for $j>s$ and $\sum_jc_j=s$. Since $s\le L/4$, the logarithm of the last expression is at least $s\log5-s/(L-s+1)\ge s$. Thus $\log D\ge n/64$ throughout this case. Equation <a href="#l:assignment-rough-index" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:assignment-rough-index">[l:assignment-rough-index]</a> now gives $I\le D^{193}$.

Suppose instead that $L>3n/4$. Orient a longest line as the top row, of length $L$, and write $\zeta$ for the lower diagram of size $s=n-L$. Assign the entire top row to its row and use the preceding arm–leg assignment on $\zeta$. Hook comparison and <a href="#l:assignment-rough-index" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:assignment-rough-index">[l:assignment-rough-index]</a> applied to the tail give $$I\le\binom nsD_\zeta e^{3s},\qquad
 D\ge\binom nsD_\zeta
              \exp\left(-\frac{s}{L-s+1}\right).$$ If $s\ge1$, then $\binom ns\ge(n/s)^s\ge4^s$ and $L-s+1\ge2$. Hence $$\log D\ge s(\log4-1/2),\qquad
 \log(I/D)\le\tfrac72s.$$ These bounds imply $I\le D^{1024}$. Transposing the assignment back preserves the subgroup index and interchanges trivial and sign factors. If $s=0$, take the full group and its trivial or sign character. Finally, for $n<64$ and $D>1$, the trivial subgroup has index $n!\le64^{64}<2^{1024}\le D^{1024}$. Degree-one representations again use the full group. This covers every case. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### From a character space to an isotypic space

The index bound is useful because a subgroup character gives an orthogonal projection through a signed average. Positivity of the original mass function controls that signed average. The next lemma also explains why repeated copies of a subgroup representation cause no loss.

<a id="stmt-l-subcharacter-isotypic"></a>
<div id="l:subcharacter-isotypic" class="lemma">

<a id="l:subcharacter-isotypic-bound"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Averaging character projections). *Let $G$ be a finite group, $H\le G$, and let $f\ge0$ have mass $z\le1$ and satisfy $f(gH)\le B/[G:H]$ for every $g\in G$. Let $\tau\in\mathop{\mathrm{Irr}}(H)$ have degree $d_\tau$. Suppose $J\le H$ has a one-dimensional unitary character $\chi$ occurring in $\tau|_J$, and put $I=[H:J]$. For $\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{Irr}}(G)$ of degree $D$, let $Q_\tau$ be the full $\tau$-isotypic projection in $\rho|_H$. Then $$\label{l:subcharacter-isotypic-bound}
 D\|\widehat f(\rho)Q_\tau\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le BzI d_\tau.$$ All copies of $\tau$ are included in $Q_\tau$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-subcharacter-isotypic"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-subcharacter-isotypic</summary>

*Proof.* Define the complex mass function $e_{J,\chi}$ to be $\overline{\chi(j)}/|J|$ on $J$ and zero elsewhere. Its Fourier transform is the orthogonal projection $P$ onto vectors satisfying $\rho(j)v=\chi(j)v$ for all $j\in J$. With $U_H,U_J$ denoting uniform probability masses on the subgroups, positivity gives $$|f*e_{J,\chi}|\le f*U_J\le I(f*U_H)\le\frac{IB}{|G|},
 \qquad \sum_g|(f*e_{J,\chi})(g)|\le z.$$ Plancherel therefore implies $D\|\widehat f(\rho)P\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le IBz$. Every conjugate of $(J,\chi)$ inside $H$ satisfies the same bound.

Average the corresponding projections, writing $\overline P=|H|^{-1}\sum_{h\in H}\rho(h)P\rho(h)^*$. On the full $H$-isotypic space $V_\eta\otimes\mathcal A_\eta$, a group-algebra element acts on $V_\eta$ tensored with the identity on the multiplicity space $\mathcal A_\eta$. Schur’s lemma thus gives $$\overline P|_{V_\eta\otimes\mathcal A_\eta}
   =\frac{\operatorname{rank}(P|_{V_\eta})}{d_\eta}I.$$ These scalars are nonnegative and the scalar for $\eta=\tau$ is at least $1/d_\tau$. Hence $\overline P\ge d_\tau^{-1}Q_\tau$. Test this positive-operator inequality against $\widehat f(\rho)^*\widehat f(\rho)$, and average the preceding Plancherel bounds. The result is <a href="#l:subcharacter-isotypic-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:subcharacter-isotypic-bound">[l:subcharacter-isotypic-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-l-character-lift"></a>
<div id="l:character-lift" class="theorem">

<a id="l:character-lift-bound"></a>
**Theorem 27** (Character-projection transfer). *Let $H_i=\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(M_i)\le S_n$, $1\le i\le b$, for disjoint nonempty sets $M_i$. Let $f\ge0$ have mass $z\le1$ and suppose $$f(gH_i)\le\frac B{[S_n:H_i]}
       \quad(g\in S_n,\ 1\le i\le b).$$ For every irreducible $\rho$ of degree $D$, $$\label{l:character-lift-bound}
 \|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le BzbC_1D^{-1+1026/b},$$ where $C_1=\sup_{m\ge1}\sum_{\tau\in\mathop{\mathrm{Irr}}(S_m)}d_\tau^{-1}$. More generally, if $a\ge0$ and every type of every $H_i$ has a one-dimensional subcharacter of index at most $d_\tau^a$, the exponent $1026/b$ in <a href="#l:character-lift-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:character-lift-bound">[l:character-lift-bound]</a> may be replaced by $(a+2)/b$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-character-lift"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-character-lift</summary>

*Proof.* For each type $\tau$ of $H_i$, Lemma <a href="#l:subcharacter-isotypic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:subcharacter-isotypic">26</a> and the assumed subcharacter give $$D\|\widehat f(\rho)Q_{i,\tau}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
       \le Bz d_\tau^{a+1}.$$ Restrict $\rho$ to the product of the $H_i$. In any occurring joint type, the product of the factor dimensions is at most $D$, including when that type has multiplicity greater than one. Some factor therefore has dimension at most $R=D^{1/b}$. The sum of the commuting projections $Q_{i,\tau}$ with $d_\tau\le R$ dominates the identity. Taking its trace against $\widehat f(\rho)^*\widehat f(\rho)$ gives $$D\|\widehat f(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le Bz\sum_{i=1}^b\sum_{d_\tau\le R}d_\tau^{a+1}
 \le BzbC_1R^{a+2}.$$ The last inequality uses $d_\tau^{a+1}\le R^{a+2}d_\tau^{-1}$. Lemma <a href="#l:small-index-character" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:small-index-character">25</a> supplies $a=1024$. ◻

</details>

For example, take $b=8192$ and $B=2$. If $f_n\le\mu_n$ has mass tending to one and $\widehat\mu_n(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})=o(1)$, then <a href="#l:character-lift-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:character-lift-bound">[l:character-lift-bound]</a> and Proposition <a href="#l:amplification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:amplification">6</a> give $\|\mu_n^{*4}-U_{S_n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\to0$. Indeed $\varepsilon=1026/8192<1/4$, and the nonlinear Plancherel sum for four factors is bounded by $(2bC_1)^4\sum_{D_\lambda>1}D_\lambda^{1-4(1-\varepsilon)}$, which tends to zero even using only the inverse-first-degree sum. This statement requires only the displayed caps, mass and sign input.

<a id="l:two-sided-section"></a>
### Joint restriction types and multiplication on two sides

For a fixed collection of disjoint block subgroups, we can retain the number of distinct joint restriction types rather than summing all small factor degrees. This gives an operator estimate by orbit spans. It also gives a product estimate when one measure is capped on left cosets and another is capped on right cosets. These two conclusions use the same type count, but different Fourier estimates.

<a id="l:joint-decomposition"></a>
Let $M_1,\ldots,M_b$ be disjoint nonempty subsets of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$, and put $H_i=\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}(M_i)$, acting trivially on the complement. On an irreducible $V_\lambda$ of $S_n$, restriction to $H=H_1\times\cdots\times H_b$ has the form $$\label{l:joint-decomposition}
 V_\lambda|_H
   =\bigoplus_{\boldsymbol\tau}
       (V_{\tau_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{\tau_b})
                       \otimes\mathcal A_{\boldsymbol\tau}.$$ The sum ranges over distinct occurring joint types; the nonzero space $\mathcal A_{\boldsymbol\tau}$ records their arbitrary multiplicities. Write $K_\lambda$ for the number of terms in this sum and $Q_{\boldsymbol\tau}$ for their orthogonal projections.

<a id="stmt-l-joint-types"></a>
<div id="l:joint-types" class="lemma">

<a id="l:joint-types-bound"></a>
<a id="l:joint-types-summability"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Counting joint types). *Put $k=n-\max(\lambda_1,\lambda'_1)$ and $M(k)=\sum_{j=0}^kp(j)$, where $p(0)=1$ and $p(j)$ is the partition number. Then $$\label{l:joint-types-bound}
 K_\lambda\le M(k)^b.$$ For every fixed $b$ and every fixed $\eta>0$, $$\label{l:joint-types-summability}
 \sum_{\lambda\vdash n:\,D_\lambda>1}
         K_\lambda^2D_\lambda^{-\eta}\longrightarrow0.$$ The statement is uniform over the disjoint sets $M_i$ and does not require them to have equal sizes.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-joint-types"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-joint-types</summary>

*Proof.* By branching, a type of $H_i$ has shape $\nu\subseteq\lambda$ and size $|M_i|$. Orient $\lambda$ so that a longest line is a row. Every such $\nu$ has at most $k$ boxes below its first row. For a fixed lower size $j$, the lower partition determines the first row because $|\nu|=|M_i|$ is fixed. There are therefore at most $M(k)$ possibilities for each factor. If the longest line was a column, transpose all diagrams. This proves <a href="#l:joint-types-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:joint-types-bound">[l:joint-types-bound]</a>; absent tuples only reduce the count.

There are at most $2p(k)$ ambient diagrams with this deficit. The partition bound $p(j)\le e^{3\sqrt j}$ gives, for a constant $C_b$, $$2p(k)M(k)^{2b}\le e^{C_b\sqrt k}\qquad(k\ge1).$$ By Theorem <a href="#l:degree-all-powers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:degree-all-powers">22</a>, $D_\lambda\ge c_1e^{c_2k}$ and, at each fixed positive $k$, the dimensions tend uniformly to infinity. Thus the contribution at deficit $k$ is bounded by $c_1^{-\eta}e^{C_b\sqrt k-\eta c_2k}$, a summable sequence, and tends to zero for fixed $k$. Dominated convergence proves <a href="#l:joint-types-summability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:joint-types-summability">[l:joint-types-summability]</a>. Multiplicities do not affect either counting step. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### An operator estimate retaining the joint-type count

<a id="stmt-l-joint-type-operator"></a>
<div id="l:joint-type-operator" class="theorem">

<a id="l:joint-type-operator-bound"></a>
**Theorem 29** (Joint-type orbit transfer). *Let $f\ge0$ be a mass function on $S_n$, of total mass $z\le1$, such that $f(gH_i)\le B/[S_n:H_i]$ for every $g,i$. With $K_\lambda$ as in <a href="#l:joint-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:joint-decomposition">[l:joint-decomposition]</a>, $$\label{l:joint-type-operator-bound}
 \|\widehat f(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
       \le\sqrt{BzK_\lambda}\,D_\lambda^{-1/2+1/b}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-joint-type-operator"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-joint-type-operator</summary>

*Proof.* First let $v$ lie in a single $H_i$-isotypic space of type $\tau_i$. The orbit span $W=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}\{\rho_\lambda(h)v:h\in H_i\}$ has dimension at most $d_{\tau_i}^2$. To see this in the presence of multiplicity, write the full $H_i$-isotypic space as $V_{\tau_i}\otimes\mathcal A$; the orbit lies in the image of the linear map $A\mapsto(A\otimes I)v$ from $\operatorname{End}(V_{\tau_i})$.

For a test vector $w$, $$|\langle w,\rho_\lambda(g)v\rangle|
    \le\|v\|\,\|P_{\rho_\lambda(g)W}w\|.$$ The squared projected norm is constant on each $gH_i$, and its uniform mean is $(\dim W/D_\lambda)\|w\|^2$: the average of the conjugate projections is scalar by Schur’s lemma, and its trace is $\dim W$. Cauchy–Schwarz for the mass function $f$, followed by its coset cap, gives $$|\langle w,\widehat f(\lambda)v\rangle|^2
 \le zB\frac{\dim W}{D_\lambda}\|v\|^2\|w\|^2
 \le\frac{zB d_{\tau_i}^2}{D_\lambda}\|v\|^2\|w\|^2.$$ Thus this single-type bound holds before any joint decomposition. In a joint type of <a href="#l:joint-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:joint-decomposition">[l:joint-decomposition]</a>, the factor degrees satisfy $\prod_i d_{\tau_i}\le D_\lambda$, since the entire tensor representation occurs at least once. Choose $i$ with $d_{\tau_i}\le D_\lambda^{1/b}$ and apply the preceding bound. For an arbitrary $v$, decompose it into its $K_\lambda$ orthogonal joint components and sum their bounds. Their norms have sum at most $\sqrt{K_\lambda}\|v\|$, proving <a href="#l:joint-type-operator-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:joint-type-operator-bound">[l:joint-type-operator-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

The following two specializations keep two useful ways of comparing the joint-type count with the ambient dimension. The four-block argument uses a uniform linear lower bound in the broad-diagram range. The eight-block argument uses a different cutoff for the longest line and a direct hook estimate below that cutoff.

<a id="stmt-l-four-block-reservoir"></a>
<div id="l:four-block-reservoir" class="corollary">

**Corollary 30** (Four-block transfer). *Partition $n$ points into four sets of size $n/4$, and let $H_i$ permute those sets. For all sufficiently large such $n$, every subprobability $f$ with $f(gH_i)\le2/[S_n:H_i]$ satisfies $$\|\widehat f(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le D_\lambda^{-1/8}
       \qquad(D_\lambda>1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-four-block-reservoir"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-four-block-reservoir</summary>

*Proof.* Theorem <a href="#l:joint-type-operator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:joint-type-operator">29</a> gives $\|\widehat f(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 \le\sqrt{2K_\lambda}D_\lambda^{-1/4}$, and Lemma <a href="#l:joint-types" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:joint-types">28</a> gives $\log(2K_\lambda)=O(\sqrt k)$. We verify directly that $\log D_\lambda/\sqrt k\to\infty$ uniformly over $k\ge1$.

Orient a longest line as the first row, of length $a=n-k$. For $1\le k\le n/3$, the tableau construction of Lemma <a href="#l:tableau-constructions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:tableau-constructions">20</a> gives $$D_\lambda\ge\binom{n-k}k\ge((n-k)/k)^k.$$ For $k$ above a fixed cutoff, divide the logarithm by $\sqrt k$ and use $(n-k)/k\ge2$; for the finitely many smaller positive $k$, the same expression tends to infinity with $n$. For $k>n/3$ and $a\le n/10$, the hook formula gives $D_\lambda\ge(n/(2ea))^n\ge(5/e)^n$. If instead $a>n/10$, retain the first row and $r=\lfloor a/2\rfloor$ lower boxes; there are enough lower boxes because $k>n/3>a/2$. Their tableaux extend to the full diagram, and the same construction gives $D_\lambda\ge\binom ar\ge2^r\ge e^{cn}$ for an absolute $c>0$ and all large $n$. This proves the asserted uniform divergence. Consequently $2K_\lambda\le D_\lambda^{1/4}$ eventually, which yields the displayed operator bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-l-eight-block-probe"></a>
<div id="l:eight-block-probe" class="corollary">

**Corollary 31** (Eight-block transfer). *Partition $n$ points into eight sets of size $n/8$, and let $H_i$ permute those sets. For all sufficiently large such $n$, every subprobability $f$ with $f(gH_i)\le2/[S_n:H_i]$ satisfies $$\|\widehat f(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le D_\lambda^{-1/4}
       \qquad(D_\lambda>1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-eight-block-probe"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-eight-block-probe</summary>

*Proof.* Here the joint-type estimate is $\sqrt{2K_\lambda}D_\lambda^{-3/8}$. For $k\le n/3$, use $K_\lambda\le M(k)^8$ and $D_\lambda\ge\binom{n-k}k$ exactly as above to get $\log(2K_\lambda)/\log D_\lambda\to0$ uniformly. For $k>n/3$, the equal block sizes give the coarser count $K_\lambda\le p(n/8)^8$, whose logarithm is $O(\sqrt n)$. Let $a$ again be the longest line. If $a\ge n^{3/4}$, retain that line and $r=\lfloor a/3\rfloor$ lower boxes, possible because $k>n/3>a/3$. Tableau extension gives $D_\lambda\ge\binom ar\ge3^r$, so $\log D_\lambda\ge c n^{3/4}$. If $a<n^{3/4}$, all hooks are at most $2n^{3/4}$, and $$D_\lambda\ge\frac{n!}{(2n^{3/4})^n}
       \ge\left(\frac{n^{1/4}}{2e}\right)^n.$$ Thus $2K_\lambda\le D_\lambda^{1/4}$ uniformly for all large $n$, and the result follows. ◻

</details>

These are exact specializations of the joint-type orbit argument, including its multiplicity treatment. For reference, the four-block proof also gives $\sum_{D_\lambda>1}D_\lambda^{-4}=o(1)$ directly: at $k\le n/3$ use the summable majorant $2e^{3\sqrt k}2^{-4k}$ and fixed-$k$ divergence; at $k>n/3$ use the linear-in-$n$ logarithmic dimension bound against $p(n)$. The eight-block proof gives the inverse-third-power statement by the same summation, with majorant $2e^{3\sqrt k}2^{-3k}$ and its $n^{3/4}$ lower bound in the remaining range. With mass and sign input, these reproduce respectively the 24-factor and 10-factor Fourier completions through Proposition <a href="#l:amplification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:amplification">6</a>: the nonlinear Plancherel exponents are respectively $-4$ and $-3$. Alternatively, a final independent probability law with zero sign mean removes the sign coefficient exactly. Its Fourier matrices have operator norm at most one, so this extra convolution preserves both nonlinear estimates. This alternative does not require small sign mean in the law supplying the coset caps.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### A product with caps in opposite orientations

For a mass function $\alpha$, a cap on $H_i g$ controls left multiplication of its Fourier matrix by an $H_i$-isotypic projector. A cap on $gH_i$ controls right multiplication. The product below places these two controlled sides together. There is no assertion that either cap implies the other.

<a id="stmt-l-two-sided"></a>
<div id="l:two-sided" class="theorem">

<a id="l:two-sided-caps"></a>
<a id="l:two-sided-bound"></a>
**Theorem 32** (Two-sided Fourier multiplication). *Let $H_1,\ldots,H_b$ be the disjoint symmetric block subgroups above. Let $\alpha,\beta\ge0$ have masses $a,c\le1$ and satisfy $$\label{l:two-sided-caps}
 \alpha(H_i g)\le\frac{B_L}{[S_n:H_i]},\qquad
 \beta(gH_i)\le\frac{B_R}{[S_n:H_i]}
       \quad(g\in S_n,\ 1\le i\le b).$$ Then every irreducible $\lambda\vdash n$ satisfies $$\label{l:two-sided-bound}
 D_\lambda\|\widehat\beta(\lambda)
                     \widehat\alpha(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le B_LB_Rac\,K_\lambda^2D_\lambda^{-1+4/b}.$$ In particular, for eight blocks and $B_L=B_R=2$, the bound is $4K_\lambda^2D_\lambda^{-1/2}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-two-sided"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-two-sided</summary>

<a id="l:two-sided-density-caps"></a>
*Proof.* Use densities $A=|S_n|\alpha$ and $B=|S_n|\beta$ with respect to uniform probability on $G=S_n$. Write $\int_G$ and $\int_{H_i}$ for normalized counting averages. The two caps read $$\label{l:two-sided-density-caps}
 \int_{H_i}A(h^{-1}g)\,dh\le B_L,
 \qquad
 \int_{H_i}B(gh^{-1})\,dh\le B_R.$$ Indeed these averages are respectively $[G:H_i]\alpha(H_i g)$ and $[G:H_i]\beta(gH_i)$. The density transform $\int_G A(g)\rho_\lambda(g)\,dg$ equals the mass transform $\widehat\alpha(\lambda)$, and similarly for $B$. These conventions give $\widehat{\beta*\alpha}=\widehat\beta\widehat\alpha$.

<a id="l:two-sided-isotypic"></a>
For a type $\tau$ of $H_i$ of degree $d_\tau$ and character $\chi_\tau$, its full isotypic projector is $$P_{i,\tau}=d_\tau\int_{H_i}
              \overline{\chi_\tau(h)}\rho_\lambda(h)\,dh.$$ Centrality makes this scalar on each irreducible carrier, and character orthogonality identifies the scalar as one on type $\tau$ and zero otherwise. It acts as the identity on all multiplicity copies of $\tau$. Consider the density $$C(g)=d_\tau\int_{H_i}\overline{\chi_\tau(h)}A(h^{-1}g)\,dh.$$ Weighted Cauchy–Schwarz and nonnegativity give $$|C(g)|^2\le d_\tau^2
  \left(\int_{H_i}A(h^{-1}g)\,dh\right)
  \left(\int_{H_i}|\chi_\tau(h)|^2A(h^{-1}g)\,dh\right).$$ The first factor is at most $B_L$. The average over $G$ of the second factor is $a\int_{H_i}|\chi_\tau|^2=a$. The Fourier matrix of $C$ is $P_{i,\tau}\widehat\alpha(\lambda)$. Plancherel, retaining the $\lambda$ term, therefore yields $$\label{l:two-sided-isotypic}
 D_\lambda\|P_{i,\tau}\widehat\alpha(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
       \le B_La d_\tau^2,
 \qquad
 D_\lambda\|\widehat\beta(\lambda)P_{i,\tau}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
       \le B_Rc d_\tau^2.$$ For the second inequality use $d_\tau\int_{H_i}\overline{\chi_\tau(h)}B(gh^{-1})\,dh$, whose transform is $\widehat\beta(\lambda)P_{i,\tau}$, and the second cap in <a href="#l:two-sided-density-caps" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:two-sided-density-caps">[l:two-sided-density-caps]</a>.

The projectors for the different disjoint factors commute, and $Q_{\boldsymbol\tau}=\prod_iP_{i,\tau_i}$ are the orthogonal joint projections in <a href="#l:joint-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:joint-decomposition">[l:joint-decomposition]</a>. On a nonzero joint space, $\prod_i d_{\tau_i}\le D_\lambda$, so some $d_{\tau_i}\le D_\lambda^{1/b}$. Since $Q_{\boldsymbol\tau}\le P_{i,\tau_i}$, the two bounds in <a href="#l:two-sided-isotypic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:two-sided-isotypic">[l:two-sided-isotypic]</a> imply $$\begin{split}
 \|\widehat\beta Q_{\boldsymbol\tau}\widehat\alpha\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
 &\le\|\widehat\beta Q_{\boldsymbol\tau}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
             \|Q_{\boldsymbol\tau}\widehat\alpha\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\\
 &\le\sqrt{B_LB_Rac}\,D_\lambda^{-1+2/b}.
 \end{split}$$ Sum over the $K_\lambda$ joint types before squaring, and then multiply by $D_\lambda$. This gives <a href="#l:two-sided-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:two-sided-bound">[l:two-sided-bound]</a>. The factor $K_\lambda^2$ is retained; no multiplicity-free restriction has been assumed. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-l-two-sided-completion"></a>
<div id="l:two-sided-completion" class="corollary">

**Corollary 33** (Two-factor completion). *Fix $b>4$ and finite constants $B_L,B_R$. Suppose probabilities $\mu_n,\nu_n$ on $S_n$ dominate subprobabilities $\alpha_n,\beta_n$, respectively, whose masses tend to one and which satisfy <a href="#l:two-sided-caps" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:two-sided-caps">[l:two-sided-caps]</a> for $b$ disjoint nonempty blocks. If $\widehat\nu_n(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})\widehat\mu_n(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}})\to0$, then $$\|\nu_n*\mu_n-U_{S_n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\longrightarrow0.$$ In particular this applies to a single law with small sign mean when separate subprobabilities supply its two cap orientations.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-two-sided-completion"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-two-sided-completion</summary>

*Proof.* Use Lemma <a href="#l:joint-types" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:joint-types">28</a> with $\eta=1-4/b>0$ in Theorem <a href="#l:two-sided" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:two-sided">32</a>. It makes the sum of the nonlinear Plancherel terms for $\beta_n*\alpha_n$ tend to zero. Its trivial coefficient is the product of the two masses, tending to one. Domination bounds the error in each sign coefficient by the corresponding lost mass, so its sign coefficient also tends to zero. Lemma <a href="#l:plancherel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:plancherel">5</a> now gives convergence to uniform in $\ell^1$. Finally, $\beta_n*\alpha_n\le\nu_n*\mu_n$ and the difference has mass one minus the product of the retained masses. Adding that mass proves the conclusion. ◻

</details>

<a id="l:equivariant-section"></a>
### Equivariant kernels and their multiplicity spaces

Partial-permutation information can be imposed on a transition kernel without first selecting a starting permutation. This preserves a group action on the entire state space. The resulting Fourier decomposition has two parts: an irreducible carrier, on which the kernel acts as the identity, and a multiplicity space, on which it may act nontrivially. The latter space can be large even when the carrier is one-dimensional. We prove two amplification results that retain these multiplicities.

Throughout this section kernels act on functions by $$(Af)(x)=\sum_y A(x,y)f(y).$$ All Hilbert–Schmidt norms use counting measure and the ordinary trace. For a finite state space $\Omega$, let $U_\Omega$ be the matrix with every entry $1/|\Omega|$. Thus $U_\Omega$ is the orthogonal projection onto the normalized constant vector. A nonnegative matrix with row and column sums at most one is a contraction on $\ell^2(\Omega)$: indeed, $$\sum_x\left|\sum_y A(x,y)f(y)\right|^2
 \le \sum_{x,y}A(x,y)|f(y)|^2\le\sum_y|f(y)|^2.$$ We will use this fact for kernels obtained by deleting entries of a doubly stochastic kernel.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Sixteen separately truncated factors

Let $A$ and $V$ be two sets of size $n$, and let $\Omega=\operatorname{Bij}(A,V)$ be the set of bijections from labels to positions. Partition $A$ into sixteen blocks $A_1,\ldots,A_{16}$, of sizes $m_i$, and write $$H_i=S_{A_i},\qquad H=\prod_{i=1}^{16}H_i.$$ These groups act on $\Omega$ on the right by composition: $x\cdot h=x\circ h$. The quotient $X_i=\Omega/H_i$ records the positions of every label outside $A_i$. Its size is $L_i=n!/m_i!$, and each of its fibers has $m_i!$ elements.

<a id="l:sixteen-cutoff"></a>
If a kernel $K$ obeys $K(xh,yh)=K(x,y)$ for $h\in H$, its quotient kernel on $X_i$ is well defined by $$q_i(a,b)=\sum_{y:\,\pi_i(y)=b}K(x,y),\qquad \pi_i(x)=a,$$ where $\pi_i:\Omega\to X_i$ is the quotient map. Equivariance under $H_i$ makes the right side independent of the representative $x$. Fix a number $\varepsilon>0$ and define a retained kernel by $$T_i(x,y)=K(x,y)\,
 \mathbf1\left\{q_i(\pi_i(x),\pi_i(y))
                         \le\frac{1+\varepsilon}{L_i}\right\}.
 \label{l:sixteen-cutoff}$$ Since $H_i$ is normal in $H$, the quotient has a right $H$-action. Equivariance of $K$ gives $q_i(ah,bh)=q_i(a,b)$ for $h\in H$. The cutoff therefore preserves the action of all of $H$.

We specify one further hypothesis on $T_i$. In the orthogonal $H_i$-isotypic decomposition $$\ell^2(\Omega)=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\widehat H_i}
                 V_\alpha\otimes\mathcal M_{i,\alpha},
 \qquad
 T_i=\bigoplus_\alpha I_{V_\alpha}\otimes T_i^\alpha,$$ $\mathcal M_{i,\alpha}$ is the full multiplicity space, including all quotient fibers. In particular $T_i^{\mathrm{sgn}}$ denotes the operator on the sign multiplicity space, not a single scalar Fourier coefficient.

<a id="stmt-l-equivariant-sixteen"></a>
<div id="l:equivariant-sixteen" class="theorem">

<a id="l:sixteen-sign-input"></a>
**Theorem 34** (Sixteen equivariant subkernels). *Consider a sequence of the preceding state spaces and partitions with $\min_i m_i\to\infty$. Let $K$ be a doubly stochastic, $H$-equivariant kernel. Suppose that, for every $i$, <a id="l:sixteen-marginal-input"></a>$$\frac1{L_i}\sum_{a\in X_i}
 \frac12\sum_{b\in X_i}|q_i(a,b)-L_i^{-1}|\le\delta,
 \label{l:sixteen-marginal-input}$$ where $\delta\to0$. Choose $\varepsilon\to0$ with $\delta/\varepsilon\to0$, form the kernels <a href="#l:sixteen-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:sixteen-cutoff">[l:sixteen-cutoff]</a>, and assume also that $$\beta_i:=\|T_i^{\mathrm{sgn}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(1\le i\le16).
 \label{l:sixteen-sign-input}$$ Then $$\frac1{|\Omega|}\sum_{x\in\Omega}
       \|K^{16}(x,\cdot)-U_\Omega(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \longrightarrow0.$$ If $K$ is equivariant under a transitive group of permutations of $\Omega$, the same conclusion holds for the maximum over starting states.*

<a id="l:sixteen-type-bounds"></a>
*More precisely, put $T=T_1\cdots T_{16}$. For a product type $\boldsymbol\alpha=(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_{16})$, set $f_i=\dim\alpha_i$ and $D=\prod_i f_i$. If $T_{i,\boldsymbol\alpha}$ is the operator on its full $H$-multiplicity space, then $$\|T_{i,\boldsymbol\alpha}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
       \le(1+\varepsilon)\frac{f_i^2}{D},\qquad
 D\|T_{1,\boldsymbol\alpha}\cdots
             T_{16,\boldsymbol\alpha}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
       \le(1+\varepsilon)^{16}D^{-13}.
 \label{l:sixteen-type-bounds}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-l-equivariant-sixteen"><summary>Proof of stmt-l-equivariant-sixteen</summary>

<a id="l:sixteen-average-tv"></a>
*Proof.* We first bound the deleted mass, then use each of the sixteen quotient caps on its corresponding factor. Write $$\gamma_i=\frac1{|\Omega|}\sum_{x,y}(K(x,y)-T_i(x,y)).$$ On an atom with $q_i(a,b)>(1+\varepsilon)/L_i$, its positive excess over uniform is at least $\varepsilon q_i(a,b)/(1+\varepsilon)$. Summing over such atoms and then averaging over $a$ gives <a id="l:sixteen-loss"></a>$$\gamma_i\le\frac{1+\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\delta=o(1).
 \label{l:sixteen-loss}$$ Every $T_i$ is row- and column-substochastic. A uniform starting distribution remains dominated by uniform after any product of these kernels. The average mass lost in $T$ is consequently at most $\Gamma=\sum_i\gamma_i$. Also $0\le T\le K^{16}$ entrywise. The triangle inequality followed by Cauchy–Schwarz over all $|\Omega|^2$ entries gives $$\frac1{|\Omega|}\sum_x
       \|K^{16}(x,\cdot)-U_\Omega(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \le \frac\Gamma2+\frac12\|T-U_\Omega\|_{\mathrm{HS}}.
 \label{l:sixteen-average-tv}$$

<a id="l:sixteen-single-hs"></a>
Fix $i$ and first decompose only under $H_i$. Each quotient fiber is a regular $H_i$-set. After choosing one representative per fiber, the block of $T_i$ between fibers $a,b$ is convolution by nonnegative weights of total mass $$t_i(a,b)=q_i(a,b)
       \mathbf1\{q_i(a,b)\le(1+\varepsilon)/L_i\}.$$ On the multiplicity space of a regular-fiber type $\alpha$, of degree $f_\alpha$, this block is a weighted sum of unitary $f_\alpha$ by $f_\alpha$ matrices. Its Hilbert–Schmidt norm is at most $\sqrt{f_\alpha}\,t_i(a,b)$. Since each row of $t_i$ has mass at most one, the cap gives $$\|T_i^\alpha\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le f_\alpha\sum_{a,b}t_i(a,b)^2
 \le(1+\varepsilon)f_\alpha.
 \label{l:sixteen-single-hs}$$

Now decompose under the whole product $H$. Its $\boldsymbol\alpha$-isotype is $$\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{16}V_{\alpha_j}\right)
                  \otimes\mathcal M_{\boldsymbol\alpha}.$$ Viewed only as an $H_i$-space, it contributes $D/f_i$ copies of $\mathcal M_{\boldsymbol\alpha}$ to the multiplicity of $\alpha_i$. Thus <a href="#l:sixteen-single-hs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:sixteen-single-hs">[l:sixteen-single-hs]</a> implies $$\frac D{f_i}\|T_{i,\boldsymbol\alpha}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le(1+\varepsilon)f_i.$$ Multiplying these inequalities and using Hilbert–Schmidt submultiplicativity proves <a href="#l:sixteen-type-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:sixteen-type-bounds">[l:sixteen-type-bounds]</a>.

For $u>0$, write $\mathcal Z_u(m)=\sum_{\alpha\in\widehat S_m}(\dim\alpha)^{-u}$. The degree estimate of Theorem <a href="#l:degree-all-powers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="l:degree-all-powers">22</a> gives $\mathcal Z_{13}(m)\to2$. The total squared Hilbert–Schmidt contribution of all product types with $D>1$ is therefore at most <a id="l:sixteen-nonscalar-sum"></a>$$(1+\varepsilon)^{16}
       \left(\prod_{i=1}^{16}\mathcal Z_{13}(m_i)-2^{16}\right)=o(1).
 \label{l:sixteen-nonscalar-sum}$$ The subtracted types are precisely the products of trivial and sign representations, since eventually every $m_i\ge2$.

It remains to handle these $2^{16}$ scalar carrier types; their multiplicity spaces have not been discarded. If a product type has a sign coordinate $i$, its $T_i$ block has squared Hilbert–Schmidt norm at most $\beta_i$ by <a href="#l:sixteen-sign-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:sixteen-sign-input">[l:sixteen-sign-input]</a>. All the other factors are operator contractions. Hence its product block has squared Hilbert–Schmidt norm at most $\beta_i$. The sum over these finitely many types tends to zero.

For the all-trivial type, let $T_{i,0}$ be its multiplicity operator and let $u=|\Omega|^{-1/2}\mathbf1$ be the constant vector in that space. The restriction of $U_\Omega$ is $P_u=uu^*$. Equation <a href="#l:sixteen-single-hs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:sixteen-single-hs">[l:sixteen-single-hs]</a> for the trivial $H_i$ type gives $\|T_{i,0}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le1+\varepsilon$, whereas $$\langle u,T_{i,0}u\rangle=1-\gamma_i.$$ Consequently <a id="l:sixteen-trivial-rank-one"></a>$$\|T_{i,0}-P_u\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =\|T_{i,0}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2+1
       -2\langle u,T_{i,0}u\rangle
 \le\varepsilon+2\gamma_i.
 \label{l:sixteen-trivial-rank-one}$$ Telescoping the product against $P_u^{16}=P_u$, with operator contractions on either side of each difference, gives $$\|T_{1,0}\cdots T_{16,0}-P_u\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
 \le\sum_{i=1}^{16}\sqrt{\varepsilon+2\gamma_i}=o(1).$$ This is a bound on the entire all-trivial multiplicity space.

The three estimates prove $\|T-U_\Omega\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\to0$, so <a href="#l:sixteen-average-tv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="l:sixteen-average-tv">[l:sixteen-average-tv]</a> proves average mixing. Finally, equivariance under a transitive group makes all row distances of $K^{16}$ from uniform equal. That additional symmetry gives the worst-start conclusion. ◻

</details>

The sign hypothesis concerns the retained factors themselves. A probabilistic construction can verify it by cancellation inside each retained fiber block.
